Okay, so I'm trying to figure out how many chords can be drawn through 23 points on a circle. Let's break this down step by step.

First off, what exactly is a chord? A chord is simply a straight line connecting any two distinct points on the circumference of a circle. So, if I have multiple points on a circle, each unique pair of these points will form a chord.

Now, I have 23 points on this circle. My goal is to determine how many unique chords can be drawn between them. Essentially, I need to find out how many unique pairs can be formed from these 23 points.

This sounds like a combination problem in mathematics because the order of selection doesn't matter here. Whether I pick point A first and then point B or vice versa, it's still the same chord AB. So combinations are perfect for this scenario since they consider each pair only once without regard to order.

The formula for combinations is:
\[
C(n, k) = \frac{n!}{k!(n - k)!}
\]
In this case, \( n = 23 \) (since there are 23 points), and \( k = 2 \) because we're choosing pairs of points to form a chord.

Plugging in the numbers:
\[
C(23, 2) = \frac{23!}{2!(23 - 2)!} = \frac{23!}{2! \cdot 21!}
\]
Simplifying the factorials, we get:
\[
\frac{23 \times 22 \times 21!}{2 \times 1 \times 21!} = \frac{23 \times 22}{2}
\]
Calculating further:
\[
\frac{506}{2} = 253
\]

So, there are **253** unique chords that can be drawn through the 23 points on the circle.
</think>

To determine how many chords can be drawn through 23 points on a circle, we use combinations to count the number of unique pairs of points. Each pair forms a chord.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify the Problem**: We need to find the number of chords formed by connecting any two distinct points out of 23 points on a circle.

2. **Use Combinations Formula**:
   \[
   C(n, k) = \frac{n!}{k!(n - k)!}
   \]
   Here, \( n = 23 \) (total points), and \( k = 2 \) (since each chord connects two points).

3. **Apply the Values**:
   \[
   C(23, 2) = \frac{23!}{2!(23 - 2)!} = \frac{23 \times 22}{2} = \frac{506}{2}
   \]

4. **Calculate the Result**:
   \[
   C(23, 2) = 253
   \]

### Final Answer

The total number of chords that can be drawn through 23 points on a circle is:

First, I'll consider arranging the girls first since they don't have any restrictions. There are 7 girls, so they can be arranged among themselves in \(7!\) ways.

Once the girls are seated, they create gaps where we can place the boys. Specifically, if there are 7 girls, there will be 8 possible gaps (one before each girl, one between each pair of girls, and one after all the girls).

Since we don't want any two boys sitting together, each boy must occupy a separate gap. Therefore, out of these 8 gaps, we need to choose 5 distinct gaps for the boys.

The number of ways to choose 5 gaps from 8 is given by the combination formula:
\[
\binom{8}{5} = \frac{8!}{5!(8-5)!}
\]

After selecting the gaps, we can arrange the 5 boys in those chosen positions. The number of ways to do this is \(5!\).

Putting it all together, the total number of seating arrangements is:
\[
7! \times \binom{8}{5} \times 5!
\]
</think>

To determine how many ways we can seat 7 girls and 5 boys in a row so that no two boys are together, let's follow these steps:

1. **Arrange the Girls First:**
   - There are 7 girls who can be arranged among themselves in \(7!\) ways.

2. **Create Gaps for Boys:**
   - Arranging the 7 girls creates 8 possible gaps where the boys can be placed (one before each girl, one between each pair of girls, and one after all the girls).

3. **Place the Boys in the Gaps:**
   - We need to choose 5 out of these 8 gaps for the boys. The number of ways to do this is given by the combination formula:
     \[
     \binom{8}{5} = \frac{8!}{5!(8-5)!}
     \]
   
4. **Arrange the Boys:**
   - Once the positions are chosen, the 5 boys can be arranged in those positions in \(5!\) ways.

Putting it all together, the total number of valid seating arrangements is:
\[
7! \times \binom{8}{5} \times 5!
\]

The first part of the problem involves determining how many ways six cards can be selected from a standard deck without considering their suits' colors—this is simply "52 choose 6," calculated as:

$\displaystyle \dbinom{52}{6} = 20,\!358,\!520$

For the second part, we are to find how many six-card hands consist entirely of red or black cards. Since each suit has 26 cards (13 red and 13 black), this translates to calculating "26 choose 6" for both colors and summing them:

$\displaystyle \dbinom{26}{6} + \dbinom{26}{6} = 460,\!460$

Thus, the answers are as follows.

**Answer:**

There are $\boxed{20358520}$ ways to select six cards from the deck without regard to color. There are $\boxed{460460}$ such hands that consist entirely of red or black cards.

Okay, let's tackle this problem step by step. The task is to find the number of different 8-letter arrangements from the word "DAUGHTER" where all the vowels don't occur together.

First, I need to identify the letters in "DAUGHTER." It has 8 letters, right? So that means we're dealing with permutations of these 8 letters. But before anything else, let's figure out which letters are vowels and which are consonants because that will help determine how we approach grouping them.

Looking at "D-A-U-G-H-T-E-R," the vowels are A, U, E. That gives us 3 vowels: A, U, E. The rest of the letters—D, G, H, T, R—are consonants, totaling 5 consonants.

Now, since we want all the vowels to not occur together, it's easier to calculate the total number of possible arrangements and then subtract those cases where the vowels are all together. This method uses the principle of complementary counting.

First, let me compute the total number of permutations without any restrictions. Since there are 8 distinct letters, the number of ways to arrange them is simply 8 factorial (8!), which is:

8! = 40320

Next, I need to find how many arrangements have all vowels together. To do this, we can treat the block of vowels as a single entity or "super letter." So instead of thinking of 3 separate vowels, think of them combined into one unit along with the 5 consonants.

This gives us a total of (5 consonants + 1 vowel block) = 6 units to arrange. The number of ways to arrange these 6 units is 6!:

6! = 720

However, within this vowel block, the vowels themselves can be arranged among themselves in different orders since they are distinct letters. There are 3 vowels (A, U, E), so their internal arrangements are 3 factorial:

3! = 6

Therefore, the total number of arrangements where all vowels are together is:

6! * 3! = 720 * 6 = 4320

Now, to find the number of valid arrangements where vowels don't occur together, subtract the above result from the total permutations:

Total valid arrangements = Total permutations - Arrangements with vowels together
= 8! - (6! * 3!)
= 40320 - 4320
= 36000

So, the final answer is 36,000 different 8-letter arrangements where all vowels don't occur together.
</think>

To find the number of valid 8-letter arrangements from "DAUGHTER" where all vowels do not occur together, we use permutations and the principle of complementary counting.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify Letters:**
   - Total letters = 8
   - Vowels (A, U, E) = 3
   - Consonants = 5

2. **Total Permutations Without Restrictions:**
   \[
   P(8) = 8! = 40320
   \]

3. **Permutations With All Vowels Together:**
   - Treat vowels as a single entity (block).
   - Total units to arrange = 5 consonants + 1 vowel block = 6 units.
   - Permute these 6 units:
     \[
     P(6) = 6! = 720
     \]
   - Arrange the vowels within their block:
     \[
     Vowels\ permutations = 3! = 6
     \]
   - Total permutations with vowels together:
     \[
     P_{together} = 6! * 3! = 720 * 6 = 4320
     \]

4. **Valid Permutations (Vowels Not Together):**
   \[
   Valid\ arrangements = Total - Togethers = 8! - 6!*3! = 40320 - 4320 = 36000
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The number of valid 8-letter arrangements where all vowels do not occur together is **36,000**.
